266
Appendix G Mapping of Tasks and Occupational Codes
Limitation to the analysis of survey data for gig workers based 
on the mapping of tasks
The mapping of gig worker tasks to occupation codes was used in two instances in the study: (a) to 
analyze data collected through the global random domain intercept technology (RDIT) survey and 
(b) as a robustness check for the estimation of the number of online gig workers. Both analyses 
have limitations. First, there are limitations to the comparison of the profile of online gig workers 
based on the survey data and the profile of workers with similar occupations as captured by labor 
force surveys. This comparison could be carried out for only 8 of the 17 countries covered by the 
global RDIT survey that had detailed data on the occupational codes of workers in their labor force 
surveys. These eight countries are Argentina, Bangladesh, India, Mexico, Pakistan, the Philippines, 
South Africa, and Tunisia. In addition, in India, we had to map the data at a three-digit level since 
we could not identify four-digit ISCO code data, which provided less precise results. Further details 
on the labor force surveys used are in appendix D.
Second, there are limitations to the extent to which the mapping of tasks and occupational codes could 
be used as a robustness check for estimation of the number of online gig workers. We attempted to 
determine the number of online gig workers in the Philippines and Vietnam using the listed occupation 
codes as a robustness check for the estimation of the number of online gig workers. Vietnam was 
chosen because the latest labor force surveys include a question about whether a worker uses the 
internet regularly for work purposes. The Philippines was included because it is the closest country 
among our sampled countries where we have labor force survey data from the same year. Since the 
occupation codes include both online gig workers and offline workers, we used information on the 
workers’ internet usage for their daily activities to refine the data. The probability of internet usage 
for each two-digit occupation cell was calculated by incorporating labor force survey data from 
Vietnam. It was then multiplied by the total number of workers within the cell to obtain the total 
number of online workers. Although online workers do not necessarily equate to gig workers, it is 
close to our objective. This approach may have an upward bias, as it likely includes non-gig workers, 
and a downward bias, as it may fail to recognize some online gig occupations that we may have 
missed in this exercise. Overall, it could be a useful tool for estimating gross figures.


---

267
Working Without Borders: The Promise and Peril of Online Gig Work
APPENDIX H 
Demand Survey Methodology
A global survey was conducted to analyze why firms hire gig workers over web-based digital labor 
platforms as well as to determine the types of tasks outsourced and trends in firms’ hiring practices 
with respect to gig workers. To ensure the diversity of the sample both in terms of firm size and 
country coverage, several distribution channels were utilized, including:
1.	 Twitter,
2.	 The PitchBook contact database, and
3.	 Other communication channels.
The sampling strategy that was applied to each of these distribution channels is presented in the 
following sections, after an overview of the overall sample characteristics. We conclude by discussing 
survey limitations.
Characteristics of the survey sample
The final survey sample consisted of 814 firms, including 364 firms that hire gig workers. The sample 
contained companies of diverse sizes (see figures H.1 to H.3). Of all the responses to the survey, 24 
percent (or 198 responses) came from solo self-employed workers, 17 percent (139) from firms with 
2 to 4 employees, 18 percent (144) from companies that employ 5 to 19 employees, another 18 
percent (150) from firms with 20 to 99 employees, and the remaining 22 percent (183) from firms 
with 100 or more employees. The share of responses from microenterprises may seem surprising; 
however, anticipating that these firms may be difficult to reach, we purposefully targeted small busi­
nesses and start-ups through the Twitter campaign (see table H.1), so that the views of such firms 
would be captured in the survey. The size of firms that hire gig workers follows a pattern similar 
to that of all the firms, although the distribution is somewhat more uniform and, as discussed in 
Chapter 5, a smaller share of firms with 100-plus employees hire workers than could be expected 
from the distribution of all firms.

Number of responses
The firms surveyed in total span 78 countries (58 that hire gig workers), with most based in Kenya, 
Nigeria, Pakistan, and República Bolivariana de Venezuela. This is due to the way Twitter’s algorithm 
works when trying to maximize the number of clicks on the ad; see the explanation in the following 
section. Hence, the survey results better reflect the views of firms based in these countries than in 
others.
Distribution through Twitter
Twitter was chosen as a suitable distribution channel for the survey because its reach is global and 
users can be targeted on the basis of various digital labor platforms they follow. The survey was 
launched through Twitter in multiple waves, with the team closely monitoring survey results and 
trying to maximize the number of responses gathered. The World Bank’s External and Corporate 
Relations team helped implement the survey by identifying the best keywords to target respondents, 
launching the ad campaign, and monitoring its progress.
Twitter ads were launched in English only and were shown to users based in 21 countries, including 
the 17 targeted in the global RDIT survey (a survey of gig workers conducted as part of this study)245 
245	Argentina, Bangladesh, China, the Arab Republic of Egypt, India, Kenya, Lebanon, Mexico, Morocco, Nigeria, Pakistan, 
the Philippines, República Bolivariana de Venezuela, the Russian Federation, South Africa, Tunisia, and Ukraine.


---

Appendix H Demand Survey Methodology
as well as the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, and Spain. The intention was to gather 
a number of responses from the same countries as in the RIWI survey to compare the supply of gig 
labor and demand for it in each of the countries. The four developed countries were targeted since 
globally, the demand for web-based gig work originates mostly from developed countries, the United 
States in particular.246 The targeting criteria used in the first wave of Twitter ads are summarized in 
Table H.1.

As illustrated in table H.1, in addition to targeting specific countries, the ads also targeted specific 
age groups and people based on the type of content they engage with. We targeted people ages 
25 or older because people younger than 25 years old are less likely to make hiring decisions at 
their firms. Follower look-alike targeting allows people with interests similar to those of a Twitter 
account’s followers to be targeted. For example, @fiverr targets people likely to be interested in 
Fiverr. Meanwhile, keywords are used to reach people on Twitter on the basis of their search queries, 
recent Tweets, and Tweets they engaged with lately. Relevant follower look-alikes and keywords 
were suggested to the research team by the World Bank’s marketing team.
The ad contained a short message asking Twitter users to fill out the survey if they hire freelancers 
(because the term “freelancer” is more common among the general public than “gig worker”). It 
was accompanied by a short video to attract more attention.
Once the first Twitter ads wave was launched, we gathered the majority of responses from Kenya, 
Nigeria, and Pakistan. This is for two reasons. First, the survey was launched in English and these three 
countries are predominantly English speaking. Second, the Twitter algorithm works to automatically 
maximize the number of clicks on the ad for the budget given. Twitter uses an “ad score” for each 
Tweet to predict how likely someone is to engage with your ad and will serve higher-ranked Tweets 
more frequently in your campaign.247 Since it is cheaper to advertise in developing countries than 
in developed ones, Twitter was more likely to show the ad where it was gaining more traction, that

To gather responses from more diverse countries, we pursued two strategies. First, we translated 
the survey and the ad into Spanish and launched a separate ad targeting only the Spanish-speaking 
countries from the original list (Argentina, Mexico, Spain, and República Bolivariana de Venezuela,). 
The remaining targeting criteria were similar to those in table H.1, with the exception that additional 
platforms popular in the Spanish-speaking world were added to the follower look-alikes if they had 
a Twitter account.248 The strategy worked to some extent: in total, the Spanish ad helped gather 209 
responses, including 29 responses from firms that hire gig workers through online platforms. However, 
most of these responses (107) were from people based in República Bolivariana de Venezuela. As 
was the case for the English ad campaign, Twitter showed the ad to more people based in República 
Bolivariana de Venezuela because the ad was gaining more traction there.
The second strategy to capture responses from more diverse countries was to stop showing the 
English advertisement in Kenya, Nigeria, and Pakistan. We reasoned that if these countries were 
removed from the list, Twitter’s algorithm would promote the ad in the remaining countries of 
­interest. However, during the week following the change, the number of survey responses received 
every day dropped significantly. Given the growing cost per response, the decision to continue 
showing the ad in Kenya, Nigeria, and Pakistan was made. The English Twitter campaign in 
248	These included Applause/Utest, Atexto, BairesDev, FreelancerViet, Milanuncios, Neobux, Preply, Proz, RedBubble, 
Remotasks, Rent a Coder, SheWorks!, SoyFreelancer, Swagbucks, Twago, and Workana.

272
Appendix H Demand Survey Methodology
total gathered 603 responses, including 196 responses from firms that hire gig workers through 
online platforms.
Distribution using the PitchBook contact database
In parallel to the Twitter campaign, the survey link was sent to 20,285 email addresses of various firms 
gathered through the PitchBook database.249 The contacts came from 144 countries, representing 
all regions of the world, although some countries contained more contacts than others (see table 
H.3). For example, more than 1,000 each were contacted in Indonesia, South Africa, United Arab 
Emirates, and the United States and only one each in Saint Helena, São Tomé and Príncipe, Slovenia, 
Sri Lanka, and Tajikistan. This reflected both the availability of contacts in the PitchBook database 
and the desire to capture responses from developed and developing countries, ideally allowing for 
country-level analysis of responses from the most targeted countries. 
For 14,083 firms, the database contained personalized information—that is, the name and email 
address of the CEO or CFO of the company. In these cases, personalized messages were sent to 
recipients to motivate their participation. For the remaining 6,202, however, only the generic company 
email (info@....com) was available, so the same generic message was sent to all these participants. 
After the initial invite to complete the survey, each firm received two more reminders roughly a 
week apart. In total, 316 responses were gathered using this distribution channel, including 122 
from firms that hire gig workers.
Distribution through other communication channels
In addition to the Twitter ad campaign and direct emails to contacts from the PitchBook database, we 
used the S4YE LinkedIn page,250 World Bank’s Jobs and Development blog,251 and various Facebook 
groups that are commonly used to hire gig workers. In total, 46 responses, including 19 responses 
from people who hire gig workers, were generated through these communication channels.
Additionally, two platforms interviewed for the purposes of the study agreed to share the link to 
the survey with the firms that use those platforms to hire gig workers. Nevertheless, this approach 
generated only two responses. Given that the questionnaire for this distribution channel was tailored 
to ask platform-specific questions, these responses were not included in the analysis with the others.
Data validation
Upon completion of data collection, survey responses were merged to create one final data set con­
taining responses from all the respondents. In total, the sample contained 1,174 responses, including 
366 from firms that hire gig workers.
Responses were then checked for (a) duplicates and (b) suspicious response patterns (responding 
to the survey in a very short time or always selecting the first question option). Following this step, 
three responses were discarded as they failed to pass the quality check. Furthermore, 352 responses 
came from people who were not working, so they were automatically disqualified from the survey. 
This resulted in a final sample of 814 firms, including 364 responses from firms that hire gig workers.

275
Working Without Borders: The Promise and Peril of Online Gig Work
Limitations
The key survey limitation is that it is based on voluntary, nonprobability sampling. Given that respon­
dents that were willing to participate in the survey may be inherently different from those who were 
not, the results of the survey should not be inferred to the broader population of firms. 
Nonprobability sampling was chosen for a few reasons. Probability sampling requires a list of all firms 
globally that hire gig workers, from which it would be possible to sample at random, and such a list 
of enterprises does not exist (which is also why weights could not be estimated for this survey252). 
Nevertheless, in theory, such a list could be created by first surveying a random sample of all firms to 
identify those which hire gig workers and subsequently sampling from the companies identified. The 
research team explored this option by attempting to draw a sample of firms from the Orbis company 
database.253 However, this option was not feasible because of the financial and time constraints of 
the study. Despite the limitations associated with nonprobability sampling, the survey draws prelim­
inary insights into why and which firms turn to digital labor platforms, highlighting questions that 
should be explored in future research.
252	The team considered the option of developing weights that would make the total survey sample representative of firms 
worldwide. However, this approach runs the risk of distorting the sample of firms that hire gig workers through online 
platforms if these firms differ from those that are not using platforms. Hence, an unweighted survey sample was used 
throughout the analysis.
253	See: https://login.bvdinfo.com/R0/Orbis.

277
Working Without Borders: The Promise and Peril of Online Gig Work
APPENDIX I 
Social Insurance for Online Gig 
Workers: Methodology Note
Research questions
1.	 What proportion of surveyed gig platform workers have pensions and social insurance coverage?
2.	 What constrains social insurance coverage for self-employed gig workers?
3.	 What emerging approaches are governments and nongovernment actors taking to extend social 
insurance to self-employed platform workers?
4.	 What can governments do to ensure social insurance for workers in nonstandard forms of 
employment, including gig platform workers?
Data sources used
Chapter 6 uses data from multiple sources described earlier in the report, including (a) a global RDIT 
survey of gig and non-gig workers (see description in appendix D); (b) surveys of digital gig workers 
on selected platforms (see description in appendix E);254 (c) interviews with management of gig plat­
forms, policy makers, financial institutions, and relevant private sector players; and (d) focus group 
discussions with participants of digital worker operations supported by the World Bank. The data 
for empirical assessment in this chapter were collected using a standard set of questions in the social 
protection module of the survey instrument (see appendix D). Chapter 6 also uses analysis of data 
collected from a survey of gig workers in Pakistan (see description in appendix E). Bank colleagues 
from the EAP Social Protection & Jobs team also shared analysis from a March–April 2022 survey 
of informal-sector workers in Indonesia, which sought to determine participation in old-age saving 
programs.
Secondary data sources include the World Bank’s Atlas of Social Protection Indicators of Resilience 
and Equity (ASPIRE), the World Development Indicators (WDI), and the United States Social Security 
Administration’s (SSA) global surveys. 
254	Due to sample size constraints, chapter 6 focused on surveys conducted on four platforms: Microworkers, Soyfreelancer, 
Truelancer, Workana.

Working Without Borders: The Promise and Peril of Online Gig Work
APPENDIX K 
Social Insurance Regulatory 
Developments in Select Countries
TABLE K.1: Regulatory developments255
Country
Description of regulatory innovations leveraging digital platforms
Chile
The government automatically deducts 10% of each electronic invoice of the self-employed 
and directs it to the pension fund of the person’s choice. Overcollection and undercollection 
are considered at the end of the year after the self-employed has filed their tax return. To ease 
the collection process, contributions are made in practice by the corporate service buyers on 
behalf of the self-employed. 
Colombia
New legislation, Law 1174 (2020), anticipates social security system contributions for platform 
workers according to hours worked. Contributions can be paid by the day or by the hour. As 
of February 2021, independent or salaried workers who earn less than minimum wage need 
to compulsorily enroll in the public social security scheme.
Croatia
Social insurance schemes cover self-employed who contribute 1.7% of monthly covered 
earnings. The minimum monthly earnings used to calculate contributions is HRK 3,047.60. 
The maximum monthly earnings used to calculate contributions is HRK 48,120. 
Czech 
Republic
Compulsory insurance contributions for self-employed of 1.2% of monthly covered declared 
earnings. The annual declared earnings used to calculate contributions are 50% of the 
difference between income and expenses in the previous calendar year. The minimum annual 
earnings used to calculate contributions are 12 times 25% of the national average monthly 
wage (full-time self-employed persons) or 12 times 10% of the national average monthly 
wage (part-time self-employed persons). The maximum annual earnings used to calculate 
contributions are 48 times the national average monthly wage. The national average monthly 
wage is CZK 29,979.
Hungary
Self-employed persons can participate in unemployment insurance. Self-employed 
contributions are 1.5% of declared monthly earnings.
India
Social Security Code 2020 provides protection to gig workers. Under the act, a gig worker 
is defined as a person who performs work or participates in a work arrangement and 
earns from such activities outside of the traditional employer-employee relationship. After 
mandatory registration, gig workers are entitled to various social security benefits, including 
life and disability insurance, accident insurance, health and maternity benefits, and old-age 
protection. 
Indonesia
In Indonesia for motorcycle taxi platforms (such as GoJek), a small amount of the taxi fare is 
deducted automatically for accident insurance. Indonesia has introduced a digital mechanism 
to securitize the new application that the country has for motorcycle taxi rides. When using 
the application, a small amount of the tariff is automatically deducted for accident insurance 
for both the driver and the passenger for the length of the trip.
255	This appendix was compiled with the support for Maho Hatayama.
(Continued)

284
Appendix K Social Insurance Regulatory Developments in Select Countries
Country
Description of regulatory innovations leveraging digital platforms
Lithuania
Platform workers are insured by social insurance for pensions, sickness, and maternity but not 
for unemployment, accidents at work, or occupational diseases.
Malaysia
The Self-Employment Social Security Scheme was introduced to provide protection for 
individuals who are self-employed under the provisions of the Self-Employment Social 
Security Act 2017. In the beginning, this scheme was compulsory for the self-employed in 
the Passenger Transportation Sector, which includes taxi, e-hailing, and bus drivers under 
the provisions of the Self-Employment Social Security Act 2017, which took effect on 1 June 
2017. Effective from 1 January 2020, the scheme has been extended to 19 other sectors. 
The coverage for self-employed individuals contributing to the scheme starts from the date 
and time the contribution is paid and recognized on the contribution payment receipt. This 
scheme provides protection for self-employed insured persons against employment injuries, 
including occupational diseases and accidents during work-related activities. 
Slovenia
Self-employed persons contribute 2% of the monthly covered declared income. The minimum 
and maximum monthly earnings used to calculate contributions are €456 and €6,384, 
respectively.
United 
States 
Various cooperatives of freelancers are involved in the insurance against major life-cycle risks. 
Besides the traditional self-employed, they also cover platform workers. The Freelancers 
Union, for instance, the largest US cooperative of this kind with 350,000 members, provides 
health, life, and disability insurance at relatively low costs for the self-employed.
Uruguay
Uber drivers in Uruguay can download a phone application that automatically deducts 
social security contributions. In Uruguay taxi drivers, including those working through 
digital platforms (such as Uber), are covered by social insurance, requiring the registration 
and payment of contributions through an easy-to-use online application. This innovation 
builds on Uruguay’s long experience with covering self-employed workers and workers 
in microenterprises through a simplified tax and contribution payment mechanism 
(monotributo). Monotax contributions are collected by the Uruguayan Social Security Institute 
(BPS), which transfers the tax share to the fiscal authority and uses the contribution share to 
finance social security benefits for members affiliated through the scheme and their families.
Source: Study team.

287
Working Without Borders: The Promise and Peril of Online Gig Work
APPENDIX M 
Measuring Gig Work through 
Nationally Representative Surveys
To measure and analyze the evolution of gig work globally, countries should start from a 
common framework.256 The International Labour Organization (ILO) 20th International Conference 
of Labor Statisticians (ICLS), from 2018 (ICLS-18), proposed a revision of the international classification 
of status in employment (ICSE-18) based on (a) the type of authority the worker can exercise in the 
job and (b) the type of economic risk to which the worker is exposed (see figure M.1). Furthermore, 
the ILO has developed a tool to help countries improve collection of survey data on status in employ­
ment that will aid in quantifying the scale of gig work (ILO 2021a). Specifically, the new tool enables 
identification of dependent contractors. The category “dependent contractors” allows identification 
of workers who are employed for profit but do not have full control over the activities of the economic 
unit for which they work.257 While the implementation of ICSE-18 will increase the complexity of the 
questionnaire as well as the number of questions, note that filters will be used and, in practice, most 
respondents will get only around two additional questions, while a few will get more.
At the country level, efforts are under way to adopt better measures of gig work. Table M.1 
presents examples of questions that some country surveys have asked to determine the extent of gig 
employment. Examples include the 2017 US Contingent Worker Supplement (CWS), the 2020 China 
Labor Force Survey, the 2019 Swiss Labor Force Survey, and others. Questions vary in complexity. 
Of the examples excerpted in table M.1, the Swiss Labor Force Survey is longest but also richer than 
others in that it probes on earnings from platform-mediated work. 
256	This appendix was prepared with Amparo Palacios-Lopez, senior economist, World Bank.
257	The resolution defines contractors as workers who (a) have registered with the tax authorities (and/or other relevant 
bodies) as a separate business unit responsible for the relevant forms of taxes, and/or who have made arrangements 
so that their employing organization is not responsible for relevant social security payments, and/or the contractual 
relationship is not subject to national labor legislation applicable to, for example, “regular employees” but who (b) hold 
explicit or implicit contracts which correspond to those of “paid employment.”

Source: ILO 2021b. 
As the international community acts to adopt a common framework, country-level practices 
could benefit from key principles. Hardie (2016) offers useful guidance to help address gig work 
measurement issues through traditional surveys. 
•	 First, concepts being measured should be clearly defined using terminology that can be understood 
by individuals and the confidentiality of the data collected should be emphasized. Surveyors should 
define the types of activities that should be captured (for example, monetary or nonmonetary) and 
define who the target population will be (for example, businesses or households and individuals). 
•	 Second, offices of national statistics should continuously engage the ILO to ensure that country 
surveys keep pace with international best practice. 
•	 Finally, countries should identify existing surveys where additional questions on the sharing econ­
omy could be asked.

297
Working Without Borders: The Promise and Peril of Online Gig Work
APPENDIX O 
Note on Funding of Platforms
Although increased venture funding indicates a growing interest in online gig platforms, 
the average funding tends to be small and skewed toward large platforms. An overall US$9.5 
billion has been invested in gig platforms alone (figure O.1). The average total funding of gig platforms 
is US$43.5 million, but the median is US$4.56 million. This indicates that few platforms have received 
large sums, while many platforms have received much smaller amounts. On average, global platforms 
have received more funding than regional/local ones, with median funding of US$5 million and US$4.55 
million, respectively. Around a quarter of regional/local platforms have received less than US$1 million 
in funding, while some global platforms have received US$1 million to US$5 million. Two-thirds of gig 
platforms in the sample are currently venture capital funded (early, mid-, and late stage), while around 
12 percent are backed by angel investors and incubators or accelerators and around 10 percent are 
backed by later-stage investments (figure O.2). More generally, funding in the sample is skewed, with 
the 10 highest-funded platforms in the database258 attracting 58 percent of total funding. Except for Zbj, 
which is based in China, all of these platforms are based in high-income countries—Australia, France, 
Israel, the United Kingdom, and the United States. However, these top-funded platforms account for 
only 26 percent of registered users among platforms for which we have funding information, high­
lighting the importance of network effects in platform-based business, where the amount of funding 
might be a less important predictor of business success than for other business models. 
The high level of merger and acquisition activities shows volatility and experimentation 
in the platform business models. A total of 44 online gig work platforms (around 14 percent) 
have been successfully acquired by or merged with other companies in the past 10 years. Twelve (4 
percent) have gone public, while the large majority (82 percent) have not been involved in any deals. 
Of platforms that were acquired or merged, 64 percent were bought by other digital businesses, 
competitors, or corporations. The platforms that were acquired or went public include leading global 
and regional platforms with large numbers of workers such as Upwork, Freelancer, Dribbble, and 
99designs. However, they also include smaller, regional platforms such as crowd-testing platform 
Test.io; design-focused Krop; Gengo, a translation platform; and Gamersensei, a coaching platform 
focusing on gaming. This shows that business models that focus on specific market niches can 
operate competitively. The majority of the deals involve corporations as the acquiring or investing 
party. Those include competitors, which was the case in the 2019 takeover of FigureEight (previously 
Crowdflower) by the global data-labeling platform Appen,259 Middle East and North Africa–focused 
Ureed’s acquisition of local competitor Nabbesh in 2020,260 and the 2021 acquisition of the high-
end creative freelance platform Working Not Working by market leader Fiverr.261 Digital giants have

is excluded from this list, as any funding received by the firm refers to its main platform LinkedIn.com as well as other 
business lines, including the LinkedIn Services platform. Source: CB Insights (2020) and PitchBook (2020) databases.

299
Working Without Borders: The Promise and Peril of Online Gig Work
APPENDIX P 
Pricing Schemes of Online Gig 
Work Platforms
Online gig work platforms are all for-profit companies, and they usually rely on a combi­
nation of fees and/or subscription plans to generate revenue. The largest platforms (such as 
Upwork and Freelancer) tend to be publicly listed companies, while most platforms are organized as 
limited-liability corporations. The revenue usually comes from fees charged on the demand side or 
supply side or both, as well as from subscription plans that grant additional features to the online 
gig workers or clients who subscribe to them. The sample of global and regional/local platforms 
examined for this study265 shows that the commissions charged to the worker generally range from 
a minimum flat fee to 20 percent (table P.1). Global platforms (such as PeoplePerHour and Upwork) 
tend to charge progressive commissions based on the lifetime earnings of the worker as well as 
additional fees like transaction or withdrawal fees (associated with certain payment mechanisms)266 
and optional fees. Such fees may be charged on regional platforms as well, although they may be 
less common (Truelancer charges a withdrawal fee). Optional fees can enable freelancers to access 
special features, such as the ability to promote their offer or bid (the “featured offers” option on 
PeoplePerHour, or “highlight bid” on Freelancer and others ), or the ability to pass tests on the plat­
form to prove competencies (the “exam” feature on Freelancer, for instance, which is available for 
US$5 to $15).267 On the client side, the service varies; on some regional platforms there may be no 
service fee charged, or sometimes it can very high. Some platforms (SheWorks! and the Kenya-based 
Wowzi) charge commissions only on the client side. Very often, the fee is less than 5 percent of the 
transaction value (as is the case on Truelancer, Workana, Freelancer, and Upwork).
Subscription plans are often used to generate more revenue and attract more workers and 
clients. On the supply side, subscription offers are usually available as monthly plans that online 
gig workers can purchase. The subscription plans can include a reduced commission charged by the 
platform (for instance, on SoyFreelancer and Truelancer) or an increased number of times a worker 
can bid for projects in a month without being charged a fee (applicable for the “bids” on Freelancer 
and the “connects” on Upwork and Workana, for instance). Visibility on the platform can be another 
added incentive. The Freelancer Plus 268 subscription plan offered by Upwork enhances the visibility of 
subscribed workers on the platform, provides them with more information about projects they bid 
on (for example, who else is bidding), and allows them to keep information on their earnings private. 
265	The platforms in this sample were selected based on the extent to which information about their fee structure was 
available online at the time of compiling this study. For additional data, a global platform not interviewed for this study 
was included in the sample, given the ample data available online about the fee structure.
266	For instance, on Freelancer.com there are no transaction fees for transactions to local bank deposits, but fees are 
charged when using PayPal, credit card, or international wire transfer.
267	The information reported Is accurate as of January 2023.
268	Upwork, Support—Freelancer Plus, https://support.upwork.com/hc/en-us/articles/211062888-Freelancer-Plus. Accessed 
January 2023.
